-statistic with Hoeffding decomposition
\widehat{\beta}_{0}-\beta^{\ast}_{0}=\bar{L}+\bar{Q}
, where
\bar{L}
and
\bar{Q}
are the linear and quadratic terms, respectively. Classical asymptotics impose bandwidth conditions that make
\bar{Q}
negligible, yielding an asymptotic linear representation. With smaller bandwidths,
\bar{Q}
becomes non-negligible, leading to a quadratic approximation where
\mathbb{V}[\widehat{\beta}_{0}]=\mathbb{V}[\bar{L}]+\mathbb{V}[\bar{Q}]
and
\mathbb{V}[\widehat{\beta}_{0}]^{-1/2}(\widehat{\beta}_{0}-\beta^{\ast}_{0})\overset{d}{\to}N(0,1)
. Using Edgeworth expansions, they derive refined distributional approximations that improve finite-sample confidence interval coverage.

Our estimator
\widehat{\psi}^{(2)}_{k}
of the estimating equation is also a
U
-statistic whose asymptotic distribution depends on quadratic terms when
k\gtrsim n
. However, the goal of
Cattaneo et al. 2025
is inference refinement (improving finite-sample coverage), while ours is bias correction to achieve rate-optimal estimation under low smoothness conditions. In future work, we can also consider such more refined higher-order distributional approximation if concerned about the finite-sample performance beyond the asymptotic distribution results in Theorem
2
.
∎

We next derive the convergence rate of the feasible estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)}
. To this end, we need the following assumption on
\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}
.

Assumption 9
.

We assume that the estimator
\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}
of
\Sigma_{k}
satisfies the following condition:

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\xi}-\xi\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\cdot\left(\|\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}-\bm{\beta}^{\ast}\|+\|\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}-b_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\right)\cdot\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}-\Sigma_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2}).

Assumption
9
ensures that estimating
\Sigma_{k}
by
\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}
does not introduce excessive bias. When
\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}
is estimated using only
\widehat{\xi}
, we have
\|\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}-\bm{\beta}^{\ast}\|\lesssim\|\widehat{\xi}-\xi\|_{\mathbb{P},2}
. Then the condition in Assumption
9
reduces to

\left(\|\widehat{\xi}-\xi\|_{\mathbb{P},2}^{2}+\|\widehat{\xi}-\xi\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\cdot\|\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}-b_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\right)\cdot\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}-\Sigma_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2}).

(12)

Under Assumption
2
, the first two terms satisfy
\|\widehat{\xi}-\xi\|_{\mathbb{P},2}^{2}\lesssim n^{-\frac{2s_{1}}{d+2s_{1}}}
and
\|\widehat{\xi}-\xi\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\cdot\|\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}-b_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\lesssim n^{-\frac{s_{1}}{d+2s_{1}}-\frac{s_{2}}{d+2s_{2}}}
. Consequently, (
12
) requires

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}-\Sigma_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim n^{-\frac{(d-2s_{1})\vee 0}{2(d+2s_{1})}}\wedge n^{-\frac{(d^{2}-4s_{1}s_{2})\vee 0}{2(d+2s_{1})(d+2s_{2})}},

which holds if the joint density
\mathsf{p}_{X,T}(\cdot,\cdot)
is sufficiently smooth. Similar assumptions appear in most recent papers related to HOIF frameworks
(
46
;
6
;
51
)
and can be relaxed using diverging-order
U
-statistic estimators, as detailed in Supplementary Material Section
S3
following
Liu et al. 2017
or
Robins et al. 2023
.

Theorem
3
below establishes the convergence rate of the feasible estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)}
using Theorem
1
together with Assumption
9
. A proof is provided in Supplementary Material Section
S7.2
.

Theorem 3
.

Suppose that for some constant
c>0
,

\|{\mathbb{E}}[\widehat{\psi}^{(2)}_{k}(\bm{\beta}_{1})]-{\mathbb{E}}[\widehat{\psi}^{(2)}_{k}(\bm{\beta}_{2})]\|\geq c\|\bm{\beta}_{1}-\bm{\beta}_{2}\|.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{\ast}\|]\lesssim\widetilde{r}_{n,\bm{\beta}}+\|\xi-\widehat{\xi}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\cdot\|b_{\bm{\beta}^{*}}-\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\cdot\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}-\Sigma_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

If we further impose Assumption
9
, then
{\mathbb{E}}[\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)}-\bm{\beta}^{\ast}\|]\lesssim\widetilde{r}_{n,\bm{\beta}}
.

The final result in this section characterizes the asymptotic normality of the feasible second-order estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)}
, which is a direct consequence of Theorem
2
and Assumption
9
.

Theorem 4
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2
, together with Assumption
9
, all conclusions in Theorem
2
continue to hold for
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)}
.

Remark 7
.

We briefly discuss how the rate of the initial estimator
\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}
impacts the results in this section. Condition (
11
) in Theorem
2
specifies the approximation power of
\bar{\phi}_{k}
for the residuals between estimated and true nuisance parameters. Suppose that
b_{\bm{\beta}}
is differentiable with respect to
\bm{\beta}
. It is natural to assume that the second-order estimator is at least as close to the truth as the sub-optimal initial estimator, i.e.,
\widetilde{r}_{n,\bm{\beta}}\lesssim\|\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}-\bm{\beta}^{\ast}\|
. Under this assumption, using the triangle inequality and a mean-value expansion in
\bm{\beta}
, we can decompose the left-hand side of condition (
11
) as

\displaystyle\ \sup_{\|\bm{\beta}-\bm{\beta}^{\ast}\|\leq\widetilde{r}_{n,\bm{\beta}}}\|\Pi^{\perp}(\widehat{\xi}-\xi\mid\bar{\phi}_{k})\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\cdot\|\Pi^{\perp}[b_{\bm{\beta}}-\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}\mid\bar{\phi}_{k}]\|_{\mathbb{P},2}

(13)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \|\Pi^{\perp}(\widehat{\xi}-\xi\mid\bar{\phi}_{k})\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\cdot\Big\{\sup_{\|\bm{\beta}-\bm{\beta}^{\ast}\|\leq\widetilde{r}_{n,\bm{\beta}}}\|\Pi^{\perp}(b_{\bm{\beta}}-b_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}\mid\bar{\phi}_{k})\|_{\mathbb{P},2}+\|\Pi^{\perp}(b_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}-\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}\mid\bar{\phi}_{k})\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\Big\}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\ \|\Pi^{\perp}(\widehat{\xi}-\xi\mid\bar{\phi}_{k})\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\cdot\Big\{\sup_{\bm{\beta}^{{\dagger}}\in\mathcal{N}}\|\Pi^{\perp}(\nabla_{\bm{\beta}}b_{\bm{\beta}^{{\dagger}}}\mid\bar{\phi}_{k})\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\cdot\|\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}-\bm{\beta}^{\ast}\|+\|\Pi^{\perp}(b_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}-\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}\mid\bar{\phi}_{k})\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\Big\}.

Here,
\mathcal{N}\coloneqq\{\bm{\beta}:\|\bm{\beta}-\bm{\beta}^{*}\|\lesssim\|\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}-\bm{\beta}^{*}\|\}
, which contains all line segments between
\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}
and any
\bm{\beta}
with
\|\bm{\beta}-\bm{\beta}^{*}\|\leq\widetilde{r}_{n,\bm{\beta}}
.
The expression (
13
) will be used in Section
3.4
to further clarify the rate requirement for
\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}
in QTE with binary treatment.
∎

Remark 8
.

As suggested by a referee, the econometrics literature contains several related proposals for bias correction in two-step semiparametric settings, including jackknife- and bootstrap-based methods
(
8
;
7
)
. In Supplementary Material Section
S5
, we use the average treatment effect (ATE) as a concrete example to compare our approach with these alternatives.
All three methods address the problem that first-stage nuisance estimation can bias the second-stage estimator. However, the sources of bias and the correction mechanisms differ.
Cattaneo et al. 2019
and
Cattaneo and Jansson 2018
aim to estimate the estimating equation
\psi(\bm{\beta})
itself, with nuisance functions estimated by linear regression and kernel methods, respectively. The resulting estimator is affected by leave-in bias due to the reuse of the same sample in both steps, particularly when the covariate dimension is large or the bandwidth is small. By contrast, we use sample splitting and focus on estimating the approximation bias defined in (
9
). Our construction accommodates generic nuisance learners, such as logistic regression, random forests, or neural networks, because we use linear approximation only for the nuisance estimation residuals
\widehat{\xi}-\xi
and
\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}-b_{\bm{\beta}}
, although to obtain sharp theoretical results we need to impose certain smoothness assumptions on
\widehat{\xi}-\xi
and
\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}-b_{\bm{\beta}}
(see Assumption
2
).

Despite these differences, there are important connections. Specifically, we show that, as
Cattaneo et al. 2019
impose (approximately) linear assumptions on the nuisance functions whereas we do so only for the residuals of the nuisance estimates, both our target parameter and that of
Cattaneo et al. 2019
reduce to a bilinear functional
(
59
;
16
)
of the form
\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}(\bm{\beta})
defined in (
10
). We show that the jackknife- and bootstrap-based (under some assumption on the scaling between
k
and
n
) bias-corrected estimators are, respectively, exactly equivalent and asymptotically equivalent to our second-order
U
-statistic estimator
\widetilde{\mathrm{B}}_{\psi,k}(\bm{\beta})
of
\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}(\bm{\beta})
. Moreover, the HOIF framework provides a systematic way to construct higher-order
U
-statistic estimators even when
\Sigma_{k}
is unknown (see Supplementary Material Section
S3
), which we believe can also motivate similar higher-order jackknife- or bootstrap-based bias reduction methods
(
47
)
.
∎

Remark 9
.

As also noted by one referee, both
Cavaliere et al. 2024
and our paper study settings where a non-negligible asymptotic bias affects the limiting distribution.
However, the way how bias is handled differs.
Cavaliere et al. 2024
consider statistics whose limiting distribution takes the form
T_{n}=\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}B_{n}+Z_{1}
, where
Z_{1}
is centered normal and
B_{n}
is a non-vanishing asymptotic bias. They construct a bootstrap analogue
T_{n}^{*}:=\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}^{*}-\widehat{\theta}_{n})
, where
\widehat{\theta}_{n}^{*}
is a bootstrap version of
\widehat{\theta}_{n}
, such that
T_{n}^{*}-\widehat{B}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{*}}}{{\to}}Z_{1}
, with
\widehat{B}_{n}
a bootstrap-based estimator of
B_{n}
. When the bootstrap fails to replicate the bias, i.e.,
\widehat{B}_{n}-B_{n}
does not converge to zero but converges in distribution to a zero-mean random variable, the standard bootstrap
p
-value

\widehat{p}_{n}:=\mathbb{P}^{*}(T_{n}^{*}\leq T_{n})=\mathbb{P}^{*}\bigl(T_{n}^{*}-\widehat{B}_{n}\leq T_{n}-\widehat{B}_{n}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}^{*}\bigl(T_{n}^{*}-\widehat{B}_{n}\leq(T_{n}-B_{n})-(\widehat{B}_{n}-B_{n})\bigr)

is no longer asymptotically uniform. Instead, its limiting distribution converges to a distribution
H
that depends on the joint limiting distribution of
(T_{n}-\widehat{B}_{n},T_{n}^{*}-\widehat{B}_{n})
but not on the unknown bias
B_{n}
itself. The key insight of
Cavaliere et al. 2024
is to apply a prepivoting transformation:
H(\widehat{p}_{n})
is asymptotically uniform. Since
H
is unknown, they estimate it via a double bootstrap to obtain
\widehat{H}_{n}
, and output the corrected
p
-value
\widetilde{p}_{n}:=\widehat{H}_{n}(\widehat{p}_{n})
. Therefore, their procedure does not directly estimate or remove the bias
B_{n}
; instead, it bypasses the bias by transforming the bootstrap
p
-value and estimates the distribution function
H
.

In contrast, our approach directly estimates the bias itself. As shown in Section
3.2
, we further decompose the first-order bias
{\mathbb{E}}[\{\widehat{\xi}(X,T)-\xi(X,T)\}\{b_{\bm{\beta}}(X,T)-\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}(X,T)\}]
into a leading bias component
\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}(\bm{\beta})
and a truncation bias
\mathrm{TB}_{\psi,k}(\bm{\beta})
by projecting the residuals of the estimated nuisance functions onto a sieve basis
\bar{\phi}_{k}
. The leading bias
\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}(\bm{\beta})
is then unbiasedly estimated by a
U
-statistic-based correction term
\widetilde{\mathrm{B}}_{\psi,k}(\bm{\beta})
when
\Sigma_{k}
is known (see Section
3.2
). Subtracting this correction term from the first-order estimating equation yields a bias-corrected estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(2)}
that achieves
\sqrt{n}
-consistency under weaker smoothness conditions than first-order methods (see Theorem
5
and the discussion in Section
3.4
).
∎

3.4
Application: QTE with a binary treatment over
Hölder
smoothness classes

In this section, we revisit Section
3.1
and specialize the general results from Theorem
1
to Theorem
4
to the QTE setting with binary treatment
T\in\{0,1\}
(see Example
2
). To keep the exposition simple, we take the target parameter
\beta_{1}^{*}
to be the
\tau^{th}
quantile of the potential outcome
Y(1)
, i.e.
\beta_{1}^{*}=\inf\{q:\mathbb{P}(Y(1)\leq q)\geq\tau\}
. Under Assumption
1
,
\beta_{1}^{*}
is identified as the unique solution to

\displaystyle\psi(\beta_{1})={\mathbb{E}}(\tau-\mathbbm{1}\{Y(1)\leq\beta_{1}\})={\mathbb{E}}\left\{\frac{T}{{\mathbb{P}}(T=1\mid X)}\cdot(\tau-\mathbbm{1}\{Y\leq\beta_{1}\})\right\}.

This example fits into our framework with the following correspondences:

\displaystyle\Gamma(Y(t),t,\beta_{1})=\left(\tau-\mathbbm{1}\{Y(t)\leq\beta_{1}\}\right)\cdot t,\quad\xi(x,t)=\frac{t}{{\mathbb{P}}(T=1\mid X=x)},

\displaystyle b_{\beta_{1}}(x,t)=t\cdot{\mathbb{E}}(\tau-\mathbbm{1}\{Y\leq\beta_{1}\}\mid X=x,T=t).

In the QTE setting, several general regularity conditions for GTMs from Section
3.3
(Assumptions
2
–
7
) can be simplified. In particular, Assumption
3
holds automatically because
T
is binary. Assumption
7
also holds for QTE under the regularity conditions stated below; see Supplementary Material Section
S2
for verification details. To make this section self-contained, we therefore omit Assumptions
3
and
7
and restate the remaining relevant conditions as follows.

Assumption 10
.

(i) The parameter space
{\mathcal{B}}
is compact and
\beta_{1}^{*}
lies in its interior;
(ii)
\beta^{\ast}_{1}
is the unique solution to
\psi(\beta_{1})=0
, and for every
\epsilon>0
,
\inf_{|\beta_{1}-\beta_{1}^{\ast}|\geq\epsilon}|\psi(\beta_{1})|>0
; and (iii)
\psi(\cdot)
continuously differentiable on
{\mathcal{B}}
and its derivative function satisfies
\nabla_{\beta_{1}}\psi(\beta^{\ast}_{1})>0
.

Assumption 11
.

(i)
\sup_{\beta_{1}\in{\mathcal{B}}}\|\widehat{b}_{\beta_{1}}(\cdot,1)\|_{\infty}\lesssim 1
. (ii)
{\mathbb{P}}(Y\leq\beta_{1}|X=x,T=1)
is Lipschitz continuous in
\beta_{1}
. (iii) For all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
,
\ c_{5}\leq\mathsf{p}_{X}(x)\leq c_{6}
.

Assumption 12
.

\xi(\cdot,1)
and
\mathsf{p}_{Y|X,T}(\beta_{1}|\cdot,T=1)
are
Hölder
smooth with indices
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
respectively, for every
\beta_{1}\in{\mathcal{B}}
. Let
s=(s_{1}+s_{2})/2
denote the average smoothness. The nuisance estimators
\widehat{\xi}
and
\widehat{b}_{\beta_{1}}
converge to
\xi
and
b_{\beta_{1}}
at the minimax optimal rates (
n^{-\frac{s_{1}}{d+2s_{1}}}
and
n^{-\frac{s_{2}}{d+2s_{2}}}
) in
L_{2}(\mathbb{P})
. Moreover, we assume
\widehat{\xi}
and
\widehat{b}_{\beta_{1,\mathsf{init}}}
belong to
Hölder
smoothness classes with smoothness indices
s_{1}>0
and
s_{2}>0
, respectively.

Theorem 5
.

For the QTE parameter
\beta_{1}^{*}=\inf\{q:\mathbb{P}(Y(1)\leq q)\geq\tau\}
, suppose that Assumptions
1
and
8
–
12
hold. Then

(i)

if
s/d>1/4
and
k=o(n)
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\beta}^{(2)}_{1}-\beta^{\ast}_{1})\overset{d}{\rightarrow}N\left(0,\{\nabla_{\beta_{1}}\psi(\beta^{\ast}_{1})\}^{-1}V_{1}(\beta^{\ast}_{1})\{\nabla_{\beta_{1}}^{\top}\psi(\beta^{\ast}_{1})\}^{-1}\right);

(ii)

if
0<s/d\leq 1/4
and
k\asymp n^{\frac{2}{1+4s/d}}
,

{\mathbb{E}}^{1/2}[|\widehat{\beta}^{(2)}_{1}-\beta^{\ast}_{1}|^{2}]\lesssim n^{-\frac{4s/d}{1+4s/d}}.

The convergence rates for QTE in Theorem
5
match those for ATE established in
Robins et al. 2016
, which shows that these rates are minimax optimal for ATE. The proof of Theorem
5
is straightforward and proceeds as follows. Under the assumptions of Theorem
5
with an appropriate choice of basis
\bar{\phi}_{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\Pi^{\perp}(\widehat{\xi}-\xi\mid\bar{\phi}_{k})\|_{\mathbb{P},2}=O(k^{-s_{1}/d}),\quad\|\Pi^{\perp}(b_{\beta_{1,\mathsf{init}}}-\widehat{b}_{\beta_{1,\mathsf{init}}}\mid\bar{\phi}_{k})\|_{\mathbb{P},2}=O(k^{-s_{2}/d}),

because the
Hölder
-
s_{2}
smoothness of
\mathsf{p}_{Y|X,T}(\beta_{1}\mid\cdot,T=1)
implies that
b_{\beta_{1}}(\cdot,1)
is also
Hölder
-
s_{2}
smooth for any
\beta_{1}\in{\mathcal{B}}
. Furthermore, a direct calculation shows that
\nabla_{\beta_{1}}b_{\beta_{1}}(\cdot,1)=-\mathsf{p}_{Y|X,T}(\beta_{1}|\cdot,T=1)
, which is also
Hölder
-
s_{2}
smooth for any
\beta_{1}\in{\mathcal{B}}
. Combining these results with (
13
), the bias of
\widehat{\beta}^{(2)}_{1}
is bounded above by

\displaystyle k^{-2s/d}\cdot|\beta_{1,\mathsf{init}}-\beta^{\ast}_{1}|+k^{-2s/d}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}o(n^{-1/2})&s/d>1/4,k=o(n),\\
O\left(n^{-\frac{4s/d}{1+4s/d}}\right)&0<s/d\leq 1/4,k\asymp n^{\frac{2}{1+4s/d}}.\end{array}\right.

Therefore, somewhat surprisingly, the higher-order estimator imposes no rate requirement on
\beta_{1,\mathsf{init}}
, as long as
\beta_{1,\mathsf{init}}
is bounded. This stands in contrast to the first-order LDML estimator, which, as discussed following Proposition
2
, requires
s_{1}/d>1/2
. We summarize these comparisons in Table
1
below.

Smoothness conditions

Rate condition for
\beta_{1,\mathsf{init}}

Our paper

\displaystyle\frac{s_{1}/d+s_{2}/d}{2}>\frac{1}{4}

\displaystyle\|\beta_{1,\mathsf{init}}-\beta^{*}_{1}\|=O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

Kallus et al. 2024

\displaystyle{s_{1}}/{d}>\frac{1}{2}
&
\displaystyle\frac{s_{1}/d}{1+2s_{1}/d}+\frac{s_{2}/d}{1+2s_{2}/d}>\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\|\beta_{1,\mathsf{init}}-\beta^{*}_{1}\|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/4})

Ai et al. 2021

\displaystyle{s_{1}}/{d}>2

\beta_{1,\mathsf{init}}
required

Table 1:
Comparison of smoothness and initial estimator rate conditions for
\sqrt{n}
-consistent estimation of the QTE parameter
\beta_{1}^{*}=\inf\{q:\mathbb{P}(Y(1)\leq q)\geq\tau\}
. Here
s_{1}
denotes the
Hölder
smoothness index of the propensity score
\xi(\cdot,1)=1/\mathbb{P}(T=1\mid X=\cdot)
, and
s_{2}
denotes the Hölder smoothness index of the conditional outcome density
\mathsf{p}_{Y|X,T}(y\mid\cdot,T=t)
for every
y
.

Remark 10
.

For ATE in Example
1
, a direct calculation yields
b_{\bm{\beta}}(x,t)=(({\mathbb{E}}(Y|X=x,T=0)-\beta_{0})\cdot(1-t),({\mathbb{E}}(Y|X=x,T=1)-\beta_{1})\cdot t)^{\top}
and
\nabla_{\bm{\beta}}b_{\bm{\beta}}(x,t)=\text{diag}(1-t,t)
. Each component of
\nabla_{\bm{\beta}}b_{\bm{\beta}}
is infinitely smooth. As a result, condition (
11
) imposes no further convergence rate requirements on the initial estimator
\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}
of
\bm{\beta}^{\ast}
.
∎

4
Higher-Order Estimators: Toward Infinite-Dimensional Parameters

For continuous or high-dimensional treatments
(
71
)
, fixed-dimensional parameters in (
1
) are often inadequate. Motivated by
Bonvini and Kennedy 2022
and
Colangelo and Lee 2026
, we extend our framework to infinite-dimensional GTMs by setting
\omega(\cdot)\equiv\mathsf{p}_{T}(\cdot)\delta_{t}(\cdot)
and letting
p\to\infty
in (
1
). One purpose of this section is to further advocate the fruitful philosophy of turning an infinite-dimensional problem into a finite but diverging-dimensional functional estimation problem
(
46
)
. For notational convenience, we denote the infinite-dimensional GTM parameter as
\beta_{t}^{*}\equiv\beta^{*}(t):{\mathcal{T}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, a function of the treatment value
T=t
. Then
\beta_{t}^{*}
solves

\int_{u\in{\mathcal{T}}}{\mathbb{E}}\{\Gamma(Y(u),u,\beta^{\ast}_{t})\}\mathsf{p}_{T}(u)\delta_{t}(u){\mathrm{d}}u\equiv 0,\ \forall\ t\in{\mathcal{T}}.

Under Assumption
1
,
\beta^{\ast}_{t}
is identified as the solution to:

\psi_{t}(\beta)\equiv\psi_{t}(\theta_{\beta})\coloneqq{\mathbb{E}}\left\{\xi(X,T)\Gamma(Y,T,\beta)\delta_{t}(T)\right\}\equiv 0,

(14)

where
\xi(x,t)=\mathsf{p}_{T}(t)/\mathsf{p}_{T|X}(t\mid x)
.
Here
\Gamma:{\mathcal{Y}}\times{\mathcal{T}}\times{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is a known one-dimensional generalized residual function. To simplify notation, we maintain the conventions of Section
2
: for any
\beta\in{\mathcal{B}}
, we define
b_{\beta}(x,t)={\mathbb{E}}\{\Gamma(Y,T,\beta)\mid X=x,T=t\}
. It is worth noting that in this section we are interested in estimating the GTM parameter at some fixed treatment value
T=t
.

4.1
Higher-order estimators for infinite-dimensional parameters

Since the Dirac delta function
\delta_{t}(\cdot)
cannot be evaluated in practice, we approximate it with a kernel function, following
Bonvini and Kennedy 2022
; see Remark
12
for details. Let
K(\cdot)
be a base kernel function and define

\displaystyle K_{t,h}(u)\coloneqq\frac{1}{h}K\left(\frac{u-t}{h}\right),\quad g(x)\coloneqq\int_{u\in{\mathcal{T}}}K_{t,h}(u)\mathsf{p}_{X,T}(x,u){\mathrm{d}}u.

We assume the kernel
K(\cdot)
satisfies the following condition, which is also imposed by
Bonvini and Kennedy 2022
.

Assumption 13
.

K(\cdot):{\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is a uniformly bounded kernel function of order
\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2}
, where
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
are positive integers specified in Assumption
15
. It satisfies
\int_{{\mathcal{T}}}K(u){\mathrm{d}}u=1
,
\int_{{\mathcal{T}}}u^{i}K(u){\mathrm{d}}u=0
for
i=1,\ldots,\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2}-1
, and
\int_{{\mathcal{T}}}u^{\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2}}K(u){\mathrm{d}}u\eqqcolon\kappa_{\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2},1}\neq 0
.
Moreover,
g(x)\in(\underline{c}_{g},\bar{c}_{g})
for any
x
and
h
, where
0<\underline{c}_{g}<\bar{c}_{g}<\infty
are two universal constants.

Intuitively,
K_{t,h}(\cdot)\to\delta_{t}(\cdot)
as
h\to 0
, so we replace
\delta_{t}(u)
in (
14
) with
K_{t,h}(u)
. Analogous to (
6
), we construct the first-order estimating equation:

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{\beta,t}^{(1)}\coloneqq{\mathbb{U}}_{n,2}\left[K_{t,h}(T_{1})\widehat{\xi}(X_{1},T_{1})\{\Gamma(Y_{1},T_{1},\beta)-\widehat{b}_{\beta_{\mathsf{init}}}(X_{1},T_{1})\}+\widehat{b}_{\beta_{\mathsf{init}}}(X_{1},T_{2})K_{t,h}(T_{2})\right].

Following the debiasing argument in Section
3.2
, we project the localized nuisance errors
\xi(\cdot,t)-\widehat{\xi}(\cdot,t)
and
b_{\beta}(\cdot,t)-\widehat{b}_{\beta_{\mathsf{init}}}(\cdot,t)
onto the dictionary
\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}(x)
with respect to the weight
g
(see Supplementary Material Section
S8.1
). This yields the oracle second-order estimating equation
\widetilde{\psi}_{t,k}^{(2)}(\beta):=\widehat{\psi}_{t}^{(1)}(\beta)-\widetilde{\mathrm{B}}_{\psi,t,k}(\beta)
, where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{B}}_{\psi,t,k}(\beta)\coloneqq{\mathbb{U}}_{n,3}\{K_{t,h}(T_{1})\widehat{\xi}(X_{1},T_{1})-K_{t,h}(T_{3})\}\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}^{\top}(X_{1})\widetilde{\Omega}_{k_{x}}^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}(X_{2})\{\Gamma(Y_{2},T_{2},\beta)-\widehat{b}_{\beta_{\mathsf{init}}}(X_{2},T_{2})\}K_{t,h}(T_{2}),

with
\widetilde{\Omega}_{k_{x}}\coloneqq{\mathbb{E}}\{K_{t,h}(T)\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}(X)\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}(X)^{\top}\}=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}g(x)\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}(x)\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}(x)^{\top}{\mathrm{d}}x
treated as known. Here
\beta_{\mathsf{init}}
is an initial estimator of
\beta^{\ast}_{t}
computed from the nuisance sample. The oracle second-order estimator
\widetilde{\beta}^{(2)}_{t}
is defined by solving
\widetilde{\psi}_{t,k}^{(2)}(\beta)=0
.

4.2
Convergence rates of higher-order estimators

To proceed, we impose a set of regularity conditions that closely parallel those for the finite-dimensional case. For brevity, we assume Assumptions
3
and
6
–
8
hold with appropriate notational adjustments. Assumptions
4
and
5
are restated below in simplified form.

Assumption 14
.

For any
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
: (i) the parameter space
{\mathcal{B}}
is compact and
\beta^{*}_{t}
is in the interior of
{\mathcal{B}}
.
(ii)
\beta^{\ast}_{t}
is the unique solution to
\psi_{t}(\beta)=0
, and
\inf_{|\beta-\beta^{\ast}_{t}|\geq\epsilon}|\psi_{t}(\beta)|>0
for every
\epsilon>0
. (iii)
\psi_{t}(\beta)
is continuously differentiable in
\beta
with
\nabla_{\beta}\psi_{t}(\beta^{\ast}_{t})>0
.

Additionally, we impose the following smoothness conditions on the nuisance functions and their estimators, which facilitate kernel smoothing and are standard in nonparametric estimation.

Assumption 15
.

The functions
t\mapsto\xi(x,t)
and
t\mapsto\mathsf{p}_{T}(t)
,
t\mapsto\widehat{\xi}(x,t)
are
\alpha_{1}
-times continuously differentiable with uniformly bounded derivatives for any
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
. Analogously,
t\mapsto b_{\beta}(x,t)
and
t\mapsto\widehat{b}_{\beta}(x,t)
are
\alpha_{2}
-times continuously differentiable with uniformly bounded derivatives for any
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
\beta\in{\mathcal{B}}
.

Theorem
6
is the main result of this section, characterizing the convergence rate of
\widetilde{\beta}^{(2)}_{t}
. To reduce clutter, define
\Delta_{b,t}(X;\beta)\coloneqq b_{\beta}(X,t)-\widehat{b}_{\beta_{\mathsf{init}}}(X,t)
and
\Delta_{\xi,t}(X)\coloneqq\xi(X,t)-\widehat{\xi}(X,t).

Theorem 6
.

Suppose that Assumptions

3
,
6
–
8
, and
13
–
15
hold. Let
\widetilde{r}_{n,\mathsf{np}}\to 0
be a diminishing sequence as
n\to\infty
. Suppose that
h(\log n)^{2}\to 0
,
(\widetilde{r}_{n,\mathsf{np}})^{\alpha_{0}}\log n\to 0
and

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\sqrt{k_{x}}}{nh}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{nh}}\right)\log n+h^{\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2}}+\sup_{\beta\in{\mathcal{B}}}\left\|\Pi_{g}^{\perp}\{\Delta_{b,t}(\beta)\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}\}\right\|_{g,2}\cdot\left\|\Pi_{g}^{\perp}(\Delta_{\xi,t}\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}})\right\|_{g,2}\lesssim\widetilde{r}_{n,\mathsf{np}}.

Then,
\widetilde{\beta}^{(2)}_{t}
is a consistent estimator of
\beta^{*}_{t}
and satisfies

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}(|\widetilde{\beta}^{(2)}_{t}-\beta^{*}_{t}|)\lesssim

\displaystyle\ \sup_{|\beta-\beta_{t}^{\ast}|\leq\widetilde{r}_{n,\mathsf{np}}}\left\|\Pi_{g}^{\perp}\{\Delta_{b,t}(\beta)\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}\}\right\|_{g,2}\left\|\Pi_{g}^{\perp}(\Delta_{\xi,t}\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}})\right\|_{g,2}+\frac{\sqrt{k_{x}}}{nh}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{nh}}+h^{\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2}}.

Here,
\Pi^{\perp}_{g}(f\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}})(\cdot)\coloneqq f(\cdot)-\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}f(x)\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}(x)^{\top}g(x){\mathrm{d}}x\widetilde{\Omega}_{k_{x}}^{-1}\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}(\cdot)
denotes the orthocomplement of the
g
-weighted projection onto
\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}
, and
\|f\|_{g,2}^{2}=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}f(x)^{2}g(x){\mathrm{d}}x
.

The proof of Theorem
6
is given in Supplementary Material Section
S8.1
and closely follows that of Theorem
2
. Using a similar decomposition, we first establish consistency by showing
{\mathbb{E}}(|\widetilde{\beta}^{(2)}_{t}-\beta^{*}_{t}|)\lesssim\widetilde{r}_{n,\mathsf{np}}\to 0
. Building on this preliminary rate, we refine the analysis to the local region
\{\beta\in{\mathcal{B}}:|\beta-\beta_{t}^{\ast}|\lesssim\widetilde{r}_{n,\mathsf{np}}\}
to obtain the final convergence rate. The key difference lies in the kernel-weighted
U
-processes introduced by the kernel function. To apply the maximal inequality from Supplementary Material Section
S6.2
, each term in the Hoeffding decomposition must be carefully controlled with bandwidth-dependent envelopes, requiring a refined and delicate argument. These technical calculations distinguish our analysis from that of
Kennedy et al. 2017
, where the target parameter is explicitly defined and does not require such
U
-process techniques.

Remark 11
.

Following the same argument in Remark
7
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{|\beta-\beta_{t}^{\ast}|\leq\widetilde{r}_{n,\mathsf{np}}}\|\Pi_{g}^{\perp}\{\Delta_{b,t}(\beta)\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}\}\|_{g,2}\|\Pi_{g}^{\perp}(\Delta_{\xi,t}\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}})\|_{g,2}\lesssim\|\Pi_{g}^{\perp}\{\Delta_{b,t}(\beta_{\mathsf{init}})\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}\}\|_{g,2}\|\Pi_{g}^{\perp}(\Delta_{\xi,t}\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}})\|_{g,2}

\displaystyle+\ \|\Pi_{g}^{\perp}\{\nabla_{\beta}b_{\beta^{{\dagger}}}(\cdot,t)\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}\}\|_{g,2}\cdot\|\beta_{\mathsf{init}}-\beta^{*}_{t}\|\cdot\|\Pi_{g}^{\perp}(\Delta_{\xi,t}\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}})\|_{g,2},

where
\beta^{\dagger}
lies between

\beta_{\mathsf{init}}
and
\beta^{\ast}_{t}
.
Assume that
\Delta_{\xi,t}(\cdot)
is
s_{1}
-
Hölder
smooth, while
\Delta_{b,t}(\cdot;\beta_{\mathsf{init}})
and
\nabla_{\beta}b_{\beta^{{\dagger}}}(\cdot,t)
are
s_{2}
-
Hölder
smooth. With an appropriate basis
\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\Pi^{\perp}(\Delta_{\xi,t}\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}})\|_{g,2}=O(k_{x}^{-s_{1}/d}),\quad\left\|\Pi_{g}^{\perp}\{\nabla_{\beta}b_{\beta^{{\dagger}}}(\cdot,t)\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}\}\right\|_{g,2}=O(k_{x}^{-s_{2}/d}),

\displaystyle\text{and}\ \|\Pi^{\perp}\{\Delta_{b,t}(\beta_{\mathsf{init}})\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}\}\|_{g,2}=O(k_{x}^{-s_{2}/d}).

Thus, if
|\beta_{\mathsf{init}}-\beta^{*}_{t}|=O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
, Theorem
6
gives

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}(|\widetilde{\beta}^{(2)}_{t}-\beta^{*}_{t}|)\lesssim

\displaystyle\ k_{x}^{-\frac{s_{1}+s_{2}}{d}}+\ \frac{\sqrt{k_{x}}}{nh}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{nh}}+h^{\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2}}.

Choosing
k_{x}\asymp nh
makes the variance of order
(nh)^{-1/2}
, reducing the rate to
(nh)^{-(s_{1}+s_{2})/d}+(nh)^{-1/2}+h^{\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2}}
. If the average smoothness satisfies
(s_{1}+s_{2})/(2d)\geq 1/4
and
h\asymp n^{-1/(2(\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2})+1)}
, we obtain the rate
n^{-(\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2})/(2(\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2})+1)}
.
∎

Remark 12
.

Our higher-order estimator
\widetilde{\beta}_{t}^{(2)}
is related to two existing proposals for average dose–response function (ADRF) estimation. First,
Colangelo and Lee 2026
develop a kernel-based DML estimator motivated by an approximate first-order influence function obtained through kernel localization around a target treatment value. Their estimator takes the form

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{CL}}=

\displaystyle\,{\mathbb{U}}_{n,1}\left\{\frac{K_{t,h}(T_{1})\{Y_{1}-\widehat{\mu}(X_{1},t)\}}{\widehat{\mathsf{p}}_{T\mid X}(T=t\mid X_{1})}+\widehat{\mu}(X_{1},t)\right\},

where
\widehat{\mu}(x,t)\coloneqq{\mathbb{E}}[Y\mid X=x,T=t]
. They establish asymptotic normality of
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{CL}}
under high-level regularity conditions, including the key product-rate requirement

\sqrt{nh}\|\widehat{\mathsf{p}}_{T\mid X}(T=t\mid\cdot)-\mathsf{p}_{T\mid X}(T=t\mid\cdot)\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\cdot\|\widehat{\mu}(\cdot,t)-\widehat{\mu}(\cdot,t)\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\to 0
. Second,
Bonvini and Kennedy 2022
propose a second-order estimator (the BK estimator) based on the truncated parameter approach of HOIFs
(
60
)
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\beta}_{\mathrm{BK}}=

\displaystyle\,{\mathbb{U}}_{n,1}\left\{\frac{K_{t,h}(T_{1})\{Y_{1}-\widehat{\mu}(X_{1},t)\}}{\widehat{\mathsf{p}}_{T\mid X}(T=t\mid X_{1})}+\widehat{\mu}(t,X_{1})\right\}

\displaystyle+\,{\mathbb{U}}_{n,2}\left\{K_{t,h}(T_{1})\{Y_{1}-\widehat{\mu}(X_{1},T_{1})\}\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}(X_{1})^{\top}\widetilde{\Omega}_{k_{x}}^{-1}\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}(X_{2})\left(\frac{K_{t,h}(T_{2})}{\widehat{\mathsf{p}}_{T\mid X}(T_{2}\mid X_{2})}-1\right)\right\}.

In essence,
\widetilde{\beta}_{\mathrm{BK}}
can be viewed as a higher-order extension of
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{CL}}
for ADRF.

Our oracle second-order estimator is obtained by solving

\displaystyle 0={\mathbb{U}}_{n,2}\left[K_{t,h}(T_{1})\widehat{\xi}(X_{1},T_{1})\{Y_{1}-\widehat{\mu}(X_{1},T_{1})-\beta+\beta_{\mathsf{init}}\}+\{\widehat{\mu}(X_{1},T_{2})-\beta_{\mathsf{init}}\}K_{t,h}(T_{2})\right]

\displaystyle+{\mathbb{U}}_{n,3}\big[\{K_{t,h}(T_{1})\widehat{\xi}(X_{1},T_{1})-K_{t,h}(T_{3})\}\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}(X_{1})^{\top}\widetilde{\Omega}_{k_{x}}^{-1}\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}(X_{2})\{Y_{2}-\widehat{\mu}(X_{2},T_{2})-\beta+\beta_{\mathsf{init}}\}K_{t,h}(T_{2})\big].

Notably, because
b_{\beta}(X,t)-\widehat{b}_{\beta_{\mathsf{init}}}(X,t)=\mu(X,t)-\beta-\widehat{\mu}(X,t)+\beta_{\mathsf{init}}
and
\Pi_{g}^{\perp}[\beta-\beta_{\mathsf{init}}\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}]\equiv 0
for any
\beta
, the convergence rate in Theorem
6
simplifies to

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{k_{x}}}{nh}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{nh}}+h^{\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2}}+\left\|\Pi_{g}^{\perp}\left[\mu(\cdot,t)-\widehat{\mu}(\cdot,t)\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}\right]\right\|_{g,2}\cdot\left\|\Pi_{g}^{\perp}\left[\Delta_{\xi,t}\mid\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}\right]\right\|_{g,2},

This rate coincides with that of Theorem 1 in
Bonvini and Kennedy 2022
, confirming that our framework generalizes their result to a broader class of nonparametric GTM parameters, including the QDRF (Example
8
) as a special case.
∎

Remark 13
.

When the unknown matrix
\widetilde{\Omega}_{k_{x}}
in
\widetilde{\psi}_{t,k}^{(2)}
is replaced by an estimator
\widehat{\Omega}_{k_{x}}
computed from the nuisance sample, we obtain the feasible estimator
\widehat{\psi}_{t,k}^{(2)}
. Correspondingly, we define the feasible estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{t}^{(2)}
for
\beta_{t}^{*}
as the solution to
\widehat{\psi}_{t,k}^{(2)}(\beta)=0
. Following similar arguments as in Theorem
3
, the convergence rate of
\widehat{\beta}_{t}^{(2)}
satisfies

{\mathbb{E}}[|\widehat{\beta}^{(2)}_{t}-\beta^{*}_{t}|]\lesssim\ {\mathbb{E}}[|\widetilde{\beta}^{(2)}_{t}-\beta^{*}_{t}|]+\|\Delta_{\xi,t}\|_{g,2}\cdot\|\Delta_{b,t}(\beta^{*}_{t})\|_{g,2}\cdot\|\widehat{\Omega}_{k_{x}}-\widetilde{\Omega}_{k_{x}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

This bound mirrors the structure established in Theorem
4
for the finite-dimensional case, and can serve as a building block for establishing asymptotic normality of the feasible estimator under appropriate conditions on
\widehat{\Omega}_{k_{x}}
.
∎

5
Numerical Experiments

5.1
Simulation studies

In this section, we conduct a proof-of-concept simulation study comparing the performance of our proposed higher-order estimator with two competing methods that do not utilize the HOIF framework. We focus on estimating the QTE parameter with binary treatment,
\bm{\beta}^{*}=(\beta_{0}^{*},\beta_{1}^{*})^{\top}
, as described in Example
2
. Recall that
\beta_{1}^{*}
(resp.
\beta_{0}^{*}
) denotes the
\tau
-quantile of the treatment (resp. control) group. We choose
\tau=25\%
in this simulation. Following
(
74
)
and
(
15
)
, we simulate data from the following two data-generating mechanisms with different numbers of baseline covariates (
d=1
for Case 1 and
d=4
for Case 2):

•

Case 1:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}X\sim\mathrm{Uniform}([-1,1]),\\
T\mid X\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(\mathrm{expit}(\eta(0.5\cdot X;s))),\\
Y(t)\mid X=(1+0.3\cdot\eta(0.5\cdot X;s))\cdot t+0.2\cdot\varepsilon,\end{array}\right.

•

Case 2:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{4})^{\top}\sim\mathrm{Uniform}([-1,1]^{4}),\\
T\mid X\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(\mathrm{expit}(\eta(0.5\cdot X_{1};s))),\\
Y(t)\mid X=(1+0.3\cdot\eta(0.5\cdot X_{1};s))\cdot t+0.2\cdot\varepsilon,\end{array}\right.

where
\varepsilon\sim N(0,1)
,
\mathrm{expit}(x)=1/(1+\exp(-x))
,
\eta(x;s)=\sum_{j\in J,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}}2^{-j(s+0.25)}w_{j,l}(x)
with
J=\{0,3,6,9,10,16\}
and
w_{j,l}(\cdot)
is the D6 father wavelet functions. By construction,
\eta(\cdot;s)
lies close to the boundary of
Hölder
-smooth functions with smoothness index
s
. We note that this is only an approximation, as exact infinite basis expansions are infeasible in simulations. Cases 1 and 2 share the same true outcome regression and propensity score. The only difference is that Case 2 includes three extra non-contributing covariates. In the analysis, we do not assume such knowledge so the basis
\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}
depends on all four covariates by stacking the univariate spline basis blocks together with an intercept term
\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(x)=(1,\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{1}}(x_{1})^{\top},\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{2}}(x_{2})^{\top},\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{3}}(x_{3})^{\top},\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{4}}(x_{4})^{\top})^{\top}
, which yields a basis vector of dimension
k=\sum_{j=1}^{4}k_{j}+1
.

We vary
s\in\{0.25,0.4,0.6\}
to examine its impact on estimator performance and to validate the theoretical results derived earlier. For each combination of sample size
n
and smoothness level
s
, we run 1000 replications. In each replication,
2n
observations are drawn and split equally into a nuisance sample and an estimation sample. We estimate
\bm{\beta}^{*}
using three methods:

HOE:

Our higher-order estimator is defined in Section
3.2
. For each continuous covariate, we use a degree-1 B-spline expansion with the number of interior knots set to
\lceil n/100\rceil
. As mentioned, we construct the basis
\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(x)
by stacking the univariate spline basis blocks for all continuous covariates, together with an intercept term. Specifically, in Case 2,
\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(x)=(1,\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{1}}(x_{1})^{\top},\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{2}}(x_{2})^{\top},\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{3}}(x_{3})^{\top},\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{4}}(x_{4})^{\top})^{\top},
with
k_{j}=\lceil n/100\rceil+1
and total dimension
k=\sum_{j=1}^{4}k_{j}+1
.
On the nuisance sample, we estimate the stabilized weight function
\widehat{\xi}
via logistic regression, obtain the initial estimator
\bm{\beta}_{\mathrm{init}}
by stabilized weighting using
\widehat{\xi}
, and estimate
\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathrm{init}}}
also via logistic regression.

GOE:

The generalized optimization estimator of
Ai et al. 2021
, applied to both the nuisance and estimation samples. See Supplementary Material Section
S9.1
for its explicit form.

LDML:

The localized debiased machine learning estimator of
Kallus et al. 2024
, implemented with the same settings as in Section 6.1 of that paper, where all nuisance functions are estimated via logistic regression.

Both Figure
3
(for Case 1) and Figure
4
(for Case 2), displaying simulation results for the QTE
\beta_{1}^{\ast}-\beta_{0}^{\ast}
, show that when the smoothness parameter
s
is low (e.g.,
s=0.25
or more precisely
s
is close to
0.25
), our higher-order estimator (HOE) substantially outperforms both GOE and LDML, achieving lower MSE and absolute bias, especially when the sample size
n
is relatively large. In particular, the higher-order estimator is empirically
\sqrt{n}
-consistent even when
s
is near 0.25 (the lower right panels of Figure
3
and Figure
4
). This advantage stems from the difficulty of estimating nuisance functions under low smoothness, which induces large first-stage bias—due to errors in both the nuisance parameter estimates and the initial estimator
\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}
. Consequently, GOE and LDML fail to achieve
\sqrt{n}
-consistency (see the lower‑right panel). As
s
increases, all three methods exhibit improvements, with bias, variance, and MSE all decreasing. These empirical findings are consistent with our theoretical results, which indicate that the higher-order estimator enjoys improved statistical properties when the
Hölder
smoothness of the nuisance parameters is low.

Figures
5
and
6
(for Case 1) and Figures
7
and
8
(for Case 2) in Supplementary Material Section
S9.1
present normal QQ-plots of our higher-order estimators for

\beta_{0}^{*}
and
\beta_{1}^{*}
across all
(n,s)
combinations. These plots support the theoretical conclusion (Theorem
4
) that the higher-order estimators are asymptotically normal under appropriate scaling.

Finally, we perform sensitivity analyses to examine whether our higher-order estimator is sensitive to the choice of
k
or to the initial estimator
\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}
. Recall that, for each continuous covariate, we use a degree-1 B-spline basis with
\lceil n/100\rceil
interior knots. To examine the sensitivity to the choice of
k
, we multiply
n/100
by a factor
\lambda\in\{0.75,1,1.25\}
, so that the number of knots varies over
\lceil\lambda n/100\rceil
, resulting in
k=d\cdot(\lceil\lambda n/100\rceil+1)+1
. Figure
9
in Supplementary Material Section
S9.1
reports the empirical bias, variance, and mean squared error for HOE QTE estimator across different values of
\lambda
for both Cases 1 and 2 with
n=2000
and
s=0.25
, based on 1000 Monte Carlo replications.
The figure shows the expected bias–variance tradeoff: as
k
increases, the empirical bias of the higher-order estimator decreases, whereas the empirical variance increases. In practice, one may choose
k
such that the point estimates do not differ as much compared to the increase in variance. Here, for both Cases 1 and 2,
\lceil n/100\rceil
is a reasonable choice. A more thorough study of how to choose
k
is left to a future work that is dedicated to the problem of constructing data-driven basis.

To assess sensitivity to the initial estimator
\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}
, we perturb it deterministically as

\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}^{(\delta)}=\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}+\delta\cdot(-1,1)^{\top},
with
\delta\in\{-0.5,-0.25,0,0.25,0.5\}
. Here, the perturbation level
\delta
controls both the magnitude and the direction of the perturbation. Figure
10
in Supplementary Material Section
S9.1
reports boxplots of the estimation error of the HOE QTE estimator across different values of
\delta
for both Cases 1 and 2, in the setting
n=2000
and
s=0.25
, based on 1000 Monte Carlo replications. The results reported in Figure
10
in Supplementary Material Section
S9.1
suggest that HOE is fairly robust to moderate perturbations in the initial estimator. However, when the perturbation becomes too large, the estimator becomes less stable, as reflected by the increased dispersion and a slightly greater number of outliers.

Figure 3
:
Results for the simulation study Case 1 of estimating the QTE,
\beta_{1}^{*}-\beta_{0}^{*}
, where
\beta_{1}^{*}
(resp.
\beta_{0}^{*}
) denotes the
25\%
-quantile of the treatment (resp. control) group, using different methods, based on 1000 Monte Carlo replications.

Figure 4
:
Results for the simulation study Case 2 of estimating QTE,
\beta_{1}^{*}-\beta_{0}^{*}
, where
\beta_{1}^{*}
(resp.
\beta_{0}^{*}
) denotes the
25\%
-quantile of the treatment (resp. control) group, using different methods, based on 1000 Monte Carlo replications.

5.2
Real data analysis

In this section, we apply our higher-order estimators to estimate the QTE of 401(k) plan eligibility on household wealth, using the Survey of Income and Program Participation (SIPP) data studied in
Benjamin 2003
;
Abadie 2003
;
Chernozhukov and Hansen 2004
;
Kallus et al. 2024
. The sample contains 9,915 observations. The treatment
T
indicates eligibility for a 401(k) plan, and the outcome
Y
is net financial assets; our goal is to assess whether 401(k) eligibility increases household saving. The dataset consists of a set of pre-treatment covariates
X
, including age, income, family size, education, marital status, two-earner status, DB pension status, IRA participation, and homeownership (see
Chernozhukov and Hansen 2004
, Section 3, for details). Recently,
Kallus et al. 2024
analyzed this dataset using LDML estimators. We implement our higher-order debiased estimators on the same dataset to provide a proof-of-concept for their application in real-world settings.

As in our simulation studies, we split the sample into two folds. With the nuisance sample, we compute the initial estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{init}}
by stabilized weighting, and estimate both the stabilized weights
\widehat{\xi}
and the generalized outcome regression model
\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}
using four learners: random forest, boosting, LASSO, and a one-hidden-layer neural network. Random forests are implemented using the R package
randomForest
, boosting using
gbm
, LASSO using
hdm
, and the one-hidden-layer neural network using
nnet
.
For LASSO, we include polynomial terms of degree 6 for age, 8 for income, 4 for education, and 2 for family size, together with all binary covariates, and all pairwise interactions among these terms, resulting in a total of 275 predictors.

In the debiasing step, we use degree-2 B-spline expansions for the continuous covariates income, age, family size, and education, with interior knot numbers
(\lceil n/200\rceil,\lceil n/200\rceil,4,2)=(25,25,4,2)
, respectively. We then construct
\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(x)
by stacking the resulting univariate spline basis blocks with the binary covariates and an intercept term. This choice accounts for the heterogeneous supports of the continuous covariates: income and age vary over wide ranges, while family size and education take values on smaller grids. It also ensures numerical stability of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}
by avoiding very small eigenvalues. For comparison, we also report LDML estimates under the same settings as Section 6.2 of
Kallus et al. 2024
(with
K=5
), using a fixed two-fold split without re-randomizing folds to maintain transparency.

Table 2
:
The QTE of 401(k) eligibility in thousand dollars estimated by LDML and higher-order estimates (HOE) using diﬀerent regression methods (LASSO, neural network, boosting, and random forests). Here
\tau\in\{25\%,50\%,75\%\}
denotes the quantile level of QTE.

Table
2
reports the point estimates of 25%, 50%, and 75% QTEs. Overall, QTE estimates are generally stable among different nuisance learners, suggesting that the bias of the LDML estimator may be relatively small in this application. The higher-order estimates are close to their LDML counterparts, further strengthening this conclusion.
Consequently, the difference between the higher-order estimator and the LDML estimator provides empirical evidence on the magnitude of the potential bias of the LDML estimator
(
16
)
.

We examine sensitivity to both the choice of
k
and the initial estimator
\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}
. To assess sensitivity to
k
, we increase the number of interior knots for age by multiplying
n/200
by a factor
\lambda\in\{0.75,1,1.25\}
while keeping other knot numbers fixed. The higher-order estimators perform reasonably well in this range, as shown in Table
3
in Supplementary Material Section
S9.2
. Stability is ensured by the numerical inversion of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}
when
k
is relatively small compared to
n
, whereas the performance degrades if
\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}
approaches singularity. Therefore, a heuristic strategy to explore in the future is to increase
k
(via knots or degree) until numerical instability occurs. Developing such data-driven rules for selecting
k
and constructing
\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}
remains an important direction.

Sensitivity to
\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}
is examined similarly to the simulation studies. We find that the higher-order estimator is generally robust to small perturbations of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{init}}
, as shown in Table
4
in Supplementary Material Section
S9.2
.

6
Concluding Remarks

In this paper, we have constructed higher-order estimators for implicitly defined parameters frequently encountered in econometrics, including average treatment effects and quantile treatment effects as special cases. By applying the HOIFs framework to implicitly defined parameters, we have contributed to the existing econometrics and causal inference literature. Our proposed estimators achieve improved convergence rates compared to first-order methods, which requires only that the initial estimator be consistent rather than imposing stringent rate conditions. For binary-treatment QTE, our estimator attains
\sqrt{n}
-consistency under the Hölder smoothness condition
s/d>1/4
, substantially relaxing the requirements of existing approaches and matching the minimal Hölder smoothness condition for the ATE to be
\sqrt{n}
-estimable. We have also extended the framework to infinite-dimensional GTMs, providing a unified approach for estimating nonparametric causal functions such as dose–response curves.

Several directions remain for future research. First, while we focused on second-order estimators assuming that
\Sigma_{k}
can be estimated sufficiently accurately, relaxing this assumption would require further advances in the theory of higher-order
U
-processes. Second, extending our framework to other complexity-reducing assumptions, such as sparsity classes
(
5
)
, remains an open question. Third, with slight modifications, our framework is also expected to be able to address endogeneity problems (including nonparametric and many weak IV models)
(
15
)
. Finally, it would be interesting to compare our higher-order debiased estimator with other related bias-correction or bias-aware methods
(
4
;
75
;
11
)
.

Supplementary Materials of “Higher-Order Debiased Estimators for General Treatment Models”

Yulin Zhang
1
, Lin Liu
2
, Zheng Zhang

1
Institute of Statistics & Big Data, Renmin University of China;

2
Institute of Natural Sciences, MOE–LSC, School of Mathematical Sciences, CMA–Shanghai, SJTU–Yale Joint Center for Biostatistics and Data Science, Shanghai Jiao Tong University

Appendix S1
Comparisons of General Treatment Models and Marginal Structural Models

In this section, we further discuss the similarities and differences between the
General Treatment Models (GTM)

(
2
)
and the
Marginal Structural Models (MSM)
(See Example
6
), a well-known class of semiparametric causal models developed by Robins and colleagues
(
19
)
. MSM was developed, in part, to overcome the curse-of-dimensionality when drawing causal conclusions from interventional or observational studies when the treatment regimes under study are complex. In its simplest form, MSM directly models the counterfactual mean
{\mathbb{E}}Y(t)
as a parameterized function
g(t;\bm{\beta}^{*})
of
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
with
\bm{\beta}^{*}\coloneqq(\beta_{0}^{*},\beta_{1}^{*})^{\top}
.

When
{\mathcal{T}}=\{0,1\}
,
g
can only be a linear model, and this linear model is saturated/nonparametric: MSM (
3
) coincides with GTM in Example
1
. For example, one can posit the following nonparametric (or saturated) linear MSM:

{\mathbb{E}}Y(t)=g(t;\bm{\beta}^{*})=\beta_{0}^{*}+\beta_{1}^{*}t.

(S1.1)

This model is nonparametric because the model imposed on
{\mathbb{E}}Y(t)
does not induce any restrictions on the observed data distribution.

When
t
is a continuous variable, however, finite-dimensional parametric MSM is often specified. For example, the statistician could insist on modeling
{\mathbb{E}}Y(t)
by linear model (
S1.1
) and believe that the model is the truth. Under unconfoundedness, linear MSM (
S1.1
) imposes the following restriction on the observed data distribution:

{\mathbb{E}}Y(t)={\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbb{E}}(Y|X,T=t)]={\mathbb{E}}[\xi(X,T)Y|T=t]=\beta_{0}^{*}+\beta_{1}^{*}t\equiv g(t;\bm{\beta}^{\ast}),

(S1.2)

where
\xi(x,t)=\mathsf{p}_{T}(x)/\mathsf{p}_{T|X}(t|x)
.
Under unconfoundedness, Model (
S1.2
) imposes the following moment/equality constraint on the data generating process:

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\xi(X,T)\left(Y-g(T;\bm{\beta}^{\ast})\right)\omega(T)\right]\equiv 0,\,\forall\,\omega:{\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}},

(S1.3)

so induces a conditional moment constraint in the sense of
Ai and Chen 2003
, leading to a non-trivial orthocomplement to the model tangent space. We also refer readers to Section 3 of
Chen and Xie 2025
for a related discussion. It should be noted that one can also parameterize the MSM using nonparametric models when
t
is continuous, as in
Bonvini and Kennedy 2022
or as we have done in Section
4
.

Appendix S2
Verification of Assumption
7
for ATE and QTE

In this section, we demonstrate that Assumptions
7
(i) and
7
(iii) hold for both ATE and QTE from Examples
1
and
2
.
We first recall the following definition from the empirical process theory
(
22
)
.

Definition 2

(VC-subgraph class)
.

A collection
{\mathcal{F}}
of measurable functions is called a VC-subgraph class, or a VC-class, if the collection of all subgraphs of the functions (see Section 2.6.2 of
van der Vaart and Wellner 2023
) in
{\mathcal{F}}
forms a VC-class of sets (see Section 2.6.1 of
van der Vaart and Wellner 2023
).

It suffices to verify Assumption
7
for the first coordinate of
\Gamma(\cdot,\cdot,\bm{\beta})
, which corresponds to the estimating equation for
\beta_{0}^{*}
when
T=0
. We first verify Assumption
7
(i):

•

ATE in Example
1
. We need only to consider the function class
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathsf{ATE}}\coloneqq\{Y-\beta_{0}:\beta_{0}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\}
, where
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
is the parameter space for
\beta_{0}^{*}
. By Lemma 2.6.17 in
(
22
)
,
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathsf{ATE}}
is of VC-subgraph with index
1
. Theorem 2.6.7 of
van der Vaart and Wellner 2023
further implies that, for any probability measure
Q
,

\mathsf{N}\left({\mathcal{F}}_{\mathsf{ATE}},\|\cdot\|_{Q,2},\epsilon\|\mathrm{F}_{\mathsf{ATE}}\|_{Q,2}\right)\leq C\,(16e)\,\epsilon^{-2},\quad 0<\epsilon<1,

where
\mathrm{F}_{\mathsf{ATE}}=\sup_{\beta_{0}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}|y-\beta_{0}|
is the envelope and
C
is a universal constant. Hence, by Definition
1
,

{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathsf{ATE}}
is of VC-type with characteristics
(4\sqrt{Ce},2\bigr)
.

•

QTE in Example
2
. In this case, the relevant function class
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathsf{QTE}}\coloneqq\{\tau-\mathbbm{1}(Y\leq\beta_{0}):\beta_{0}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\}
. Denote
\rho^{\prime}(u)\coloneqq\tau-\mathbbm{1}(u\leq 0)
, for
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then
\rho^{\prime}(\cdot)
is monotone and
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathsf{QTE}}=\rho^{\prime}\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathsf{ATE}}\coloneqq\{\rho^{\prime}(f):f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathsf{ATE}}\}
. Lemma 9.9 (viii) of
Kosorok 2008
implies that applying a monotone function to a VC-subgraph class does not increase the VC index. Therefore,
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathsf{QTE}}
is also of VC-subgraph with index
1
, and thus of VC-type with characteristics
(4\sqrt{Ce},2\bigr)
.

For verification of Assumption
7
(iii), denote the first coordinate of
\Gamma(\cdot,\cdot,\bm{\beta})
as
\Gamma^{(0)}(\cdot,\cdot,\beta_{0})
.

•

ATE in Example
1
. The difference is
\Gamma^{(0)}(Y,T,\beta_{0})-\Gamma^{(0)}(Y,T,\beta_{0}^{\prime})=(\beta_{0}-\beta_{0}^{\prime})(1-T)
, which is linear in
\beta_{0}-\beta_{0}^{\prime}
. Thus,
\Gamma^{(0)}(\cdot,\cdot,\beta_{0})
meets this condition with index
\alpha_{0}=1
.

•

QTE in Example
2
. We have
\Gamma^{(0)}(Y,T,\beta_{0})-\Gamma^{(0)}(Y,T,\beta_{0}^{\prime})=[\mathbbm{1}(Y\leq\beta_{0}^{\prime})-\mathbbm{1}(Y\leq\beta_{0})](1-T)
. Then,

\displaystyle\ {\mathbb{E}}\Big[\sup_{|\beta_{0}-\beta_{0}^{\prime}|\lesssim\delta}\big|\Gamma^{(0)}(Y,T,\beta_{0})-\Gamma^{(0)}(Y,T,\beta_{0}^{\prime})\big|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ {\mathbb{E}}\Big[(1-T)^{2}\cdot\sup_{|\beta_{0}-\beta_{0}^{\prime}|\lesssim\delta}\mathbbm{1}(\beta_{0}\wedge\beta_{0}^{\prime}<Y\leq\beta_{0}\vee\beta_{0}^{\prime})\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ {\mathbb{E}}\Big[\sup_{|\beta_{0}-\beta_{0}^{\prime}|\lesssim\delta}\mathbbm{1}(\beta_{0}\wedge\beta_{0}^{\prime}<Y\leq\beta_{0}\vee\beta_{0}^{\prime})\Big]\cdot{\mathbb{P}}(T=0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ {\mathbb{E}}\Big[\sup_{|\beta_{0}-\beta_{0}^{\prime}|\lesssim\delta}\left|\mathbb{P}_{Y}(\beta_{0})-\mathbb{P}_{Y}(\beta_{0}^{\prime})\right|\Big]\cdot{\mathbb{P}}(T=0)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\ \delta,

where
\mathbb{P}_{Y}(\beta)\coloneqq\mathbb{P}(Y\leq\beta)
is the marginal distribution of
Y
and the last inequality holds if
\mathbb{P}_{Y}(\cdot)
is Lipschitz continuous. Thus, in this case,
\Gamma^{(0)}(\cdot,\cdot,\beta_{0})
satisfies this condition with index
\alpha_{0}=1/2
.

Appendix S3
Higher-Order Estimators for Parameters of GTMs

As mentioned in Remark
5
, for the sake of completeness, the following lemma characterizes the HOIFs of
\psi(\bm{\beta})
(or more precisely, of
\bar{\psi}_{k}(\bm{\beta})
) in the sense of
Robins et al. 2016
, which paves the way towards further reducing the bias due to estimating
\Sigma_{k}
by
\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}
. However, deriving the statistical properties of these higher-order estimators is beyond the scope of this paper, as the proof will eventually need to handle
U
-processes of order growing at a rate poly-log in sample size
n
.

Lemma 1
.

For any
\bm{\beta}\in{\mathcal{B}}
, under Assumption
1
, the
m^{th}
-order influence functions of
\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}
for
m\geq 2
are of the following forms:

\displaystyle\mathsf{IF}_{\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}}^{(2)}(\bm{\beta})=-(\widehat{\xi}(X_{1},T_{1})-\xi(X_{1},T_{1}))\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{1},T_{1})^{\top}\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{2},T_{2})(\Gamma(Y_{2},T_{2},\bm{\beta})-\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}(X_{2},T_{2})),

\displaystyle\mathsf{IF}_{\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}}^{(m)}(\bm{\beta})-\mathsf{IF}_{\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}}^{(m-1)}(\bm{\beta})=

\displaystyle+(-1)^{m+1}(\widehat{\xi}(X_{1},T_{1})-\xi(X_{1},T_{1}))\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{1},T_{1})^{\top}\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\prod_{s=3}^{m}\left\{(\bar{\phi}(X_{s},T_{s})\bar{\phi}(X_{s},T_{s})^{\top}-\Sigma_{k})\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\right\}

\displaystyle\times\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{2},T_{2})(\Gamma(Y_{2},T_{2},\bm{\beta})-\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}(X_{2},T_{2})).

Then the
m^{th}
-order influence functions of
\bar{\psi}_{k}(\bm{\beta})
for
m\geq 2
simply take the forms

\displaystyle\mathsf{IF}_{\bar{\psi}_{k}}^{(m)}(\bm{\beta})=\mathsf{IF}_{\psi}^{(1)}(\bm{\beta})+\mathsf{IF}_{\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}}^{(m)}(\bm{\beta}).

Since (
1
) is only used for defining the treatment effect parameter
\bm{\beta}^{\ast}
and there is no restriction on the observed data tangent space, the derivations of HOIFs of
\bar{\psi}_{k}(\bm{\beta})
will follow the strategy presented in
Liu et al. 2024
and
Robins et al. 2016
. When referring to
Liu et al. 2024
in the proof, we always mean the
arXiv version
of
Liu et al. 2024
.

Proof of Lemma
1
.

We first derive the HOIFs of
\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}(\bm{\beta})
and
\bar{\psi}_{k}(\bm{\beta})
. To ease presentation, we introduce some additional notation useful locally in the derivation.

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}\coloneqq\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{\xi}(X,T)-\xi(X,T))\bar{\phi}_{k}(X,T)],\eta_{\bm{\beta},k}\coloneqq\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[(\Gamma(Y,T,\bm{\beta})-\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}(X,T))\bar{\phi}_{k}(X,T)],

\displaystyle\bar{\xi}_{k}(\cdot,\cdot)\coloneqq\widehat{\xi}(\cdot,\cdot)-\alpha_{k}^{\top}\bar{\phi}_{k}(\cdot,\cdot),\bar{b}_{\bm{\beta},k}(\cdot,\cdot)\coloneqq\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}(\cdot,\cdot)+\eta_{\bm{\beta},k}^{\top}\bar{\phi}_{k}(\cdot,\cdot).

So
\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}(\bm{\beta})\equiv\alpha_{k}^{\top}\Sigma_{k}\eta_{\bm{\beta},k}
and
\bar{\psi}_{k}(\bm{\beta})=\widehat{\psi}^{(1)}(\bm{\beta})-\alpha_{k}^{\top}\Sigma_{k}\eta_{\bm{\beta},k}
. By Lemma A.2 of
Liu et al. 2024
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{IF}_{\alpha_{k}}^{(1)}=\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\left(\bar{\xi}_{k}(X,T)-\xi(X,T)\right)\bar{\phi}_{k}(X,T),

\displaystyle\mathsf{IF}_{\eta_{\bm{\beta},k}}^{(1)}=\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\left(\Gamma(Y,T,\bm{\beta})-\bar{b}_{\bm{\beta},k}(X,T)\right)\bar{\phi}_{k}(X,T).

It is a standard calculation to show that the influence function of
\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}(\bm{\beta})
is:

\displaystyle\ \mathsf{IF}_{\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}}^{(1)}(\bm{\beta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (\widehat{\xi}(X,T)-\xi(X,T))\bar{\phi}_{k}(X,T)^{\top}\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\eta_{\bm{\beta},k}+\alpha_{k}^{\top}\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\bar{\phi}_{k}(X,T)(\Gamma(Y,T,\bm{\beta})-\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}(X,T))

\displaystyle-\alpha_{k}^{\top}\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\bar{\phi}(X,T)\bar{\phi}(X,T)^{\top}\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\eta_{\bm{\beta},k}-\alpha_{k}^{\top}\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\eta_{\bm{\beta},k}.

By applying the same calculus rule from page 50 to page 51 of
Liu et al. 2024
, we obtain the second-order influence function of
\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}
takes the form

\displaystyle\mathsf{IF}_{\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}}^{(2)}(\bm{\beta})=-(\widehat{\xi}(X_{1},T_{1})-\xi(X_{1},T_{1}))\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{1},T_{1})^{\top}\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{2},T_{2})(\Gamma(Y_{2},T_{2},\bm{\beta})-\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}(X_{2},T_{2})).

The HOIFs of
\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}(\bm{\beta})
then read as follows: for
j\geq 3

\displaystyle\ \mathsf{IF}_{\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}}^{(j)}(\bm{\beta})-\mathsf{IF}_{\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}}^{(j-1)}(\bm{\beta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (-1)^{j-1}(\widehat{\xi}(X_{1},T_{1})-\xi(X_{1},T_{1}))\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{1},T_{1})^{\top}\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\prod_{s=3}^{j}\left\{\left(\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{s},T_{s})\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{s},T_{s})^{\top}\Sigma_{k}\right)\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\right\}

\displaystyle\times\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{2},T_{2})(\Gamma(Y_{2},T_{2},\bm{\beta})-\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}(X_{2},T_{2})).

The HOIFs of
\bar{\psi}_{k}(\bm{\beta})
follows from the HOIFs of
\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}(\bm{\beta})
and the linearity of influence functions.
∎

Armed with these results, we can construct higher-order estimators of
\psi(\bm{\beta})
and of
\bm{\beta}
itself as follows. Similar to the first-order estimator of
\psi(\bm{\beta})
, which is a second-order
U
-statistic, the
m
-th order estimator of
\psi(\bm{\beta})
is given by the following
(m+1)
-th order
U
-statistic:

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{k}^{(m)}(\bm{\beta})\coloneqq\widehat{\psi}^{(1)}(\bm{\beta})+{\mathbb{U}}_{n,m+1}[\widehat{\mathsf{IF}}_{\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}}^{(m)}]

where
\widehat{\mathsf{IF}}_{\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}}^{(m)}
is defined in the same way as
\mathsf{IF}_{\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}}^{(m)}
, except that (i)
\xi(X_{1},T_{1})\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{1},T_{1})
is replaced by
\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{1},T_{3})
, (ii)
\prod\limits_{s=3}^{m}
becomes
\prod\limits_{s=4}^{m+1}
and (iii)
\Sigma_{k}
is replaced by
\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}
. It is worth noting that
-\widehat{\mathrm{B}}_{\psi,k}(\bm{\beta})\equiv{\mathbb{U}}_{n,3}[\widehat{\mathsf{IF}}_{\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}}^{(2)}(\bm{\beta})]
.
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(m)}
is then defined as the solution to the system of
U
-statistic equations

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{k}^{(m)}(\bm{\beta})\equiv 0.

Next, the following result bounds the bias and variance of
\widehat{\psi}_{k}^{(m)}(\bm{\beta})
as an estimator of the truncated parameter
\bar{\psi}_{k}(\bm{\beta})
, indicating the potential benefit of using a higher-order estimator to debias the estimation error due to estimating
\Sigma_{k}
. The proof follows immediately from
Liu et al. 2017
, hence omitted.

Lemma 2
.

For any
\bm{\beta}\in{\mathcal{B}}
and fixed positive integer
m\geq 2
, we have

\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[\widehat{\psi}_{k}^{(m)}(\bm{\beta})-\bar{\psi}_{k}(\bm{\beta})\right]\right\|\lesssim\|\widehat{\xi}-\xi\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\cdot\|\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}-b_{\bm{\beta}}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\cdot\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}-\Sigma_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{m-1}.

(S3.1)

As a consequence of Assumption
6
(ii), we in turn have

\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[\widehat{\psi}_{k}^{(m)}(\bm{\beta})-\bar{\psi}_{k}(\bm{\beta})\right]\right\|\lesssim\|\widehat{\xi}-\xi\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\cdot\left(\|\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}-\bm{\beta}\|+\|\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}-b_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\right)\cdot\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}-\Sigma_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{m-1}.

(S3.2)

If we further impose the same conditions as in Theorem
1
and additionally assume (i)
m\lesssim\sqrt{\log n}
and
k\lesssim n/\log^{3}(n)
and (ii)
\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}-\Sigma_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
, we also have

\begin{split}\mathrm{cov}[\widehat{\psi}_{k}^{(m)}(\bm{\beta})]\preceq\frac{C}{n}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\dfrac{k}{n}+\left\{\|\Pi[\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}-b_{\bm{\beta}}|\bar{\phi}_{k}]\|_{\mathbb{P},2}^{2}+\|\Pi[\widehat{\xi}-\xi|\bar{\phi}_{k}]\|_{\mathbb{P},2}^{2}\right\}\\
+\,\underset{(p,q):p,q\geq 1,\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1}{\min}\|\Pi[\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}-b_{\bm{\beta}}|\bar{\phi}_{k}]\|_{\mathbb{P},p}^{2}\cdot\|\Pi[\widehat{b}_{\bm{\beta}_{\mathsf{init}}}-b_{\bm{\beta}}|\bar{\phi}_{k}]\|_{\mathbb{P},q}^{2}\end{array}\right),\end{split}

(S3.3)

for some absolute constant
C>0
.

Lemma
2
can be served as the starting point of deriving statistical properties of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(m)}
, were results related to higher-order
U
-processes established. We leave this important technical problem to future work.

S3.1
Convergence Rates of the Empirical Gram Matrix Estimator

When
\bm{\beta}^{*}
is
n^{-1/2}
-estimable, we can appeal to the methodology developed in
Liu et al. 2017
and take
k=o(n)
or more precisely
k\lesssim n/\log^{3}(n)
without assuming regularity conditions on the marginal density
\mathsf{p}_{X}(x)
of
X
. Specifically, we use the same nuisance sample for estimating the nuisance parameters to construct the following second-order
U
-statistic estimator of
\Sigma_{k}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}\{\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}(X_{i})\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}(X_{i})^{\top}\}\otimes\{\bar{{\mathsf{w}}}_{k_{t}}(T_{j})\bar{{\mathsf{w}}}_{k_{t}}(T_{j})^{\top}\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{i},T_{j})\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{i},T_{j})^{\top}.

Obviously,
{\mathbb{E}}[\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}]=\Sigma_{k}
so it is an unbiased estimator of
\Sigma_{k}
. We note that throughout the article
\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}
denotes a generic estimator of
\Sigma_{k}
and only in this section
\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}
denotes the empirical Gram matrix estimator.

Let
\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{i},T_{j})\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{i},T_{j})^{\top}\eqqcolon Q_{i}\otimes R_{j}
to further simplify the presentation. Corollary 3.1 of
Minsker and Wei 2019
, which we cite below, provides a user-friendly moment bound on the second-order degenerate
U
-statistic component of the following Hoeffding decomposition:

\displaystyle\ \widehat{\Sigma}_{k}-\Sigma_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{n}\left\{{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{Q_{i}\otimes R_{j}+Q_{j}\otimes R_{i}}{2}|O_{i}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}Q\otimes{\mathbb{E}}R\right\}

\displaystyle+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\left\{(Q_{i}\otimes R_{j}+Q_{j}\otimes R_{i})-{\mathbb{E}}(Q_{i}\otimes R_{j}+Q_{j}\otimes R_{i}|O_{i})\right\}

\displaystyle\eqqcolon

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}G(O_{i})+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}H(O_{i},O_{j}),

where
G
and
H
are appropriately chosen
k\times k
-symmetric matrix
-valued functions. For more recent development on tail bounds for matrix-valued
U
-statistics, we refer readers to
Bandeira et al. 2025
.

Lemma 3

(Corollary 3.1 of
Minsker and Wei 2019
)
.

Let
\{O_{i}^{(l)},i=1,\cdots,n\}
for
l=1,2
be two independent copies of
\{O_{i},i=1,\cdots,n\}
and let
{\mathbb{E}}_{l}
denote the expectation with respect to only the copies indexed by
l
. Then

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}H(O_{i},O_{j})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim&\ (\log k)^{2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\sum_{j:j\neq i}{\mathbb{E}}_{2}H^{2}(O_{i}^{(1)},O_{j}^{(2)})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\right)\\
&+(\log k)^{3}\left({\mathbb{E}}\max_{i}\left\|\sum_{j:j\neq i}H^{2}(O_{i}^{(1)},O_{j}^{(2)})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\right).\end{split}

(S3.4)

Thus to obtain a useful bound of
{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}H(O_{i},O_{j})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}
, we need to control each of the two terms on the RHS of (
S3.4
), respectively denoted as
(\log k)^{2}I_{1}
and
(\log k)^{3}I_{2}
here. We first simplify the following term that will be useful to control both
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
. Then

\displaystyle\ \sum_{j:j\neq i}H^{2}(O_{i}^{(1)},O_{j}^{(2)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{n^{2}(n-1)^{2}}\sum_{j:j\neq i}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\{Q_{i}^{(1)}\otimes(R_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}R)\}\{Q_{i}^{(1)\top}\otimes(R_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}R)^{\top}\}\\
+\,\{(Q_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}Q)\otimes R_{i}^{(1)}\}\{(Q_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}Q)^{\top}\otimes R_{i}^{(1)\top}\}\\
+\,\{Q_{i}^{(1)}\otimes(R_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}R)\}\{(Q_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}Q)^{\top}\otimes R_{i}^{(1)\top}\}\\
+\,\{(Q_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}Q)\otimes R_{i}^{(1)}\}\{Q_{i}^{(1)\top}\otimes(R_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}R)^{\top}\}\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{n^{2}(n-1)}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\{Q_{i}^{(1)}Q_{i}^{(1)\top}\}\otimes\{\frac{1}{n-1}\sum\limits_{j:j\neq i}(R_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}R)(R_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}R)^{\top}\}\\
+\,\{\frac{1}{n-1}\sum\limits_{j:j\neq i}(Q_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}Q)(Q_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}Q)^{\top}\}\otimes\{R_{i}^{(1)}R_{i}^{(1)\top}\}\\
+\,\frac{1}{n-1}\sum\limits_{j:j\neq i}\{Q_{i}^{(1)}(Q_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}Q)^{\top}\}\otimes\{(R_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}R)R_{i}^{(1)\top}\}\\
+\,\frac{1}{n-1}\sum\limits_{j:j\neq i}\{(Q_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}Q)Q_{i}^{(1)\top}\}\otimes\{R_{i}^{(1)}(R_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}R)^{\top}\}\end{array}\right\}.

As for
I_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\ \sum_{j:j\neq i}{\mathbb{E}}_{2}H^{2}(O_{i}^{(1)},O_{j}^{(2)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{n^{2}(n-1)}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\{Q_{i}^{(1)}Q_{i}^{(1)\top}\}\otimes{\mathbb{E}}_{2}(R_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}R)(R_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}R)^{\top}\\
+{\mathbb{E}}_{2}(Q_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}Q)(Q_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}Q)^{\top}\otimes\{R_{i}^{(1)}R_{i}^{(1)\top}\}\\
+\,{\mathbb{E}}_{2}\{Q_{i}^{(1)}(Q_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}Q)^{\top}\}\otimes\{(R_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}R)R_{i}^{(1)\top}\}\\
+{\mathbb{E}}_{2}\{(Q_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}Q)Q_{i}^{(1)\top}\}\otimes\{R_{i}^{(1)}(R_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}R)^{\top}\}\end{array}\right\}.

The first two terms in the above display are relatively easy to control. For the last two terms, we use the third term as an example and by symmetry the fourth term follows from the same analysis.

\displaystyle\ {\mathbb{E}}_{2}\{Q_{i}^{(1)}(Q_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}Q)^{\top}\}\otimes\{(R_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}R)R_{i}^{(1)\top}\}={\mathbb{E}}_{2}\{Q_{i}^{(1)}\otimes(R_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}R)\}\{(Q_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}Q)\otimes R_{i}^{(1)}\}^{\top}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ Q_{i}^{(1)}R_{i}^{(1)\top}\otimes{\mathbb{E}}_{2}(R_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}R)(Q_{j}^{(2)}-{\mathbb{E}}R)^{\top}.

Then

\displaystyle I_{1}\lesssim\frac{{\mathbb{E}}\|QQ^{\top}\|_{\mathrm{op}}k_{t}+{\mathbb{E}}\|RR^{\top}\|_{\mathrm{op}}k_{x}+{\mathbb{E}}\|QR^{\top}\|_{\mathrm{op}}+{\mathbb{E}}\|RQ^{\top}\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{n(n-1)}\lesssim\frac{k}{n^{2}}.

As for
I_{2}
, we have, by repeatedly applying the matrix Bernstein inequality as in Lemma
4
,

\displaystyle I_{2}\equiv

\displaystyle\ {\mathbb{E}}\max_{i}\left\|\sum_{j:j\neq i}H^{2}(O_{i}^{(1)},O_{j}^{(2)})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\ \frac{k}{n^{3}}.

Thus combining the above together, we have that the second-order degenerate
U
-statistic part is bounded by

\displaystyle\left(\frac{k\log k}{n}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\sqrt{k}\log k}{n}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{k^{1/3}\log k}{n}\right)^{3}.

As for the linear term in the above decomposition, one can directly apply the standard matrix Bernstein inequality
(
21
)
, cited below.

Lemma 4

(Rephrasing Theorem 6.1.1 of
Tropp 2015
)
.

Let
M\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}G(O_{i})
. If for each
i=1,\cdots,n
,
\|G(O_{i})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim k/n
, then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\|M\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{k\log k}{n}}+\frac{k\log k}{n}.

Thus in summary, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}-\Sigma_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{k\log k}{n}}+\frac{k\log k}{n}+\left(\frac{k\log k}{n}\right)^{2}+k\left\{\left(\frac{\log k}{n}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\log k}{n}\right)^{3}\right\}.

We can then combine this result with the empirical HOIF framework developed in
Liu et al. 2017
to construct higher-order estimating equations for
\bm{\beta}^{*}
as:

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{k}^{(m)}(\bm{\beta})=0,

such that the final estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{(m)}
with order
m\asymp\log n
is
n^{-1/2}
-consistent and asymptotic normal with no extra smoothness assumption imposed on the marginal density of
X
.

Appendix S4
Discussion of Assumption
8

In this section, we briefly show how the localized conditions in Assumption
8
imply the standard uniform eigenvalue bounds for the Gram matrices:

Lemma 5
.

For every
k_{x}
and
k_{t}
, the eigenvalues of
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}(X)^{\otimes 2}\right]
and
{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{{\mathsf{w}}}_{k_{t}}(T)^{\otimes 2}]

are bounded from above and away from zero uniformly in
k_{x}
and
k_{t}
.

Proof.

We focus on
{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{{\mathsf{w}}}_{k_{t}}(T)^{\otimes 2}]
; the argument for
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k_{x}}(X)^{\otimes 2}\right]
is analogous.
For any unit vector
{\bm{b}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k_{t}}
, by definition

\displaystyle{\bm{b}}^{\top}{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{{\mathsf{w}}}_{k_{t}}(T)^{\otimes 2}]{\bm{b}}=\int_{{\mathcal{T}}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k_{t}}b_{i}{\mathsf{w}}_{i}(t)\right)^{2}\mathsf{p}_{T}(t){\mathrm{d}}t.

By Assumption
8
(ii), for any
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
, the number of nonzero terms in the basis function
\bar{{\mathsf{w}}}_{k_{t}}(t)
is at most
c_{8}
. Denoted them
by
({\mathsf{w}}_{i_{1}}(t),\ldots,{\mathsf{w}}_{i_{c_{8}}}(t))^{\top}
. Then,

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k_{t}}b_{i}{\mathsf{w}}_{i}(t)\right)^{2}\lesssim\sum_{j=1}^{c_{8}}b_{i_{j}}^{2}{\mathsf{w}}_{i_{j}}(t)^{2}.

It follows from Assumptions
8
(i) and (iv) that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{T}}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k_{t}}b_{i}{\mathsf{w}}_{i}(t)\right)^{2}\mathsf{p}_{T}(t){\mathrm{d}}t\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{T}}}\sum_{j=1}^{c_{8}}b_{i_{j}}^{2}{\mathsf{w}}_{i_{j}}(t)^{2}\mathsf{p}_{T}(t){\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{c_{8}}b_{i_{j}}^{2}\int_{{\mathcal{S}}_{T,i_{j}}}\zeta_{t}(k_{t})^{2}\mathsf{p}_{T}(t){\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{c_{8}}b_{i_{j}}^{2}\cdot k_{t}\cdot 1/k_{t}\lesssim 1,

which yields the desired uniform upper bound on the largest eigenvalue.

For the lower bound, note that
\sum_{i=1}^{k_{t}}\mathbbm{1}\{t\in{\mathcal{S}}_{T,i}\}\leq c_{8}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k_{t}}\int_{{\mathcal{S}}_{T,i}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k_{t}}b_{i}{\mathsf{w}}_{i}(t)\right)^{2}\mathsf{p}_{T}(t){\mathrm{d}}t=

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{T}}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k_{t}}\mathbbm{1}\{t\in{\mathcal{S}}_{T,i}\}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k_{t}}b_{i}{\mathsf{w}}_{i}(t)\right)^{2}\mathsf{p}_{T}(t){\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ c_{8}\int_{{\mathcal{T}}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k_{t}}b_{i}{\mathsf{w}}_{i}(t)\right)^{2}\mathsf{p}_{T}(t){\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\ {\bm{b}}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{{\mathsf{w}}}_{k_{t}}(T)\bar{{\mathsf{w}}}_{k_{t}}(T)^{\top}\right]{\bm{b}}.

Then taking sum over all
{\mathcal{S}}_{T,i}
and using the assumption that
{\bm{b}}^{\top}\int_{{\mathcal{S}}_{X,i}}\bar{{\mathsf{w}}}_{k_{t}}(t)\bar{{\mathsf{w}}}_{k_{t}}(t)^{\top}{\mathrm{d}}t{\bm{b}}\gtrsim b_{i}^{2}
, we obtain the lower bound

\displaystyle{\bm{b}}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{{\mathsf{w}}}_{k_{t}}(T)\bar{{\mathsf{w}}}_{k_{t}}(T)^{\top}\right]{\bm{b}}\gtrsim 1.

∎

Appendix S5
On the Relationship Between Higher-Order Estimators and Other Related Proposals in Econometrics

As suggested by a referee, in this section, we illustrate the connection between the higher-order estimators studied in this paper and several other bias correction methods in the recent econometrics literature, including
Cattaneo et al. 2019
,
Cattaneo and Jansson 2018
, and
Cattaneo et al. 2025
.

S5.1
Discussion of
Cattaneo et al. 2019

Cattaneo et al. 2019
study two-step semiparametric estimation and inference in settings where the first-step linear regression may include many covariates. To keep the exposition simple, we focus on the ATE case and take the target parameter to be
\beta^{*}={\mathbb{E}}[Y(1)]
. To facilitate comparison with our construction, we characterize
\beta^{*}
via the moment condition

\displaystyle\psi(\beta^{*})=\mathbb{E}\left[\xi(X,T)\{Y-b(X,T=1)\}+b(X,T=1)-\beta^{\ast}\right]=0,

(S5.5)

where
\xi(x,t)=t/\mathbb{P}(T=1|X=x)\
and
\ b(x,t)=t{\mathbb{E}}[Y|X=x,T=1]
are nuisance functions.

In this example, we first would like to emphasize that
Cattaneo et al. 2019
and our paper target different parameters, which will be discussed in detail shortly after. However,
Cattaneo et al. 2019
and our paper share a common feature, which is that both consider to use linear models (of a possibly nonlinear
k
-dimensional feature map/basis
\bar{\phi}_{k}
of
(X,T)
) to approximate certain quantities related to nuisance parameters, such that the target parameters of
Cattaneo et al. 2019
and ours both reduce to bilinear functionals, with their precise forms to be revealed thereafter. In particular,
Cattaneo et al. 2019
use linear models to directly approximate the nuisance parameters, whereas we use a linear approximation to represent the nuisance estimation
errors
; when the nuisance estimates are zeros, these two approaches collapse. For such bilinear functionals, a naïve plug-in estimator can have non-negligible leave-in bias in high-dimensional settings when the dimension
k\gtrsim\sqrt{n}

(
8
)
. In terms of bias correction strategies,
Cattaneo et al. 2019
start from the plug-in estimator and apply a jackknife-based correction, whereas we exploit the bilinear structure directly and construct an unbiased
U
-statistic estimator. We show below that, for bilinear functionals, these two strategies yield the same estimator.

To facilitate the analysis below, let
\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(X)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
be a dictionary for
X
. Since
T
is binary, define
\bar{\phi}_{k}(X,T)=T\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(X)
and the
k\times k
population Gram matrix
\Sigma_{k}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\big[\bar{\phi}_{k}(X,T)^{\otimes 2}\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[T\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(X)^{\otimes 2}\big]
.

Strictly following
Cattaneo et al. 2019
, they are interested in solving for
\beta^{*}
of the estimating equation
\psi(\beta^{*})=0
in (
S5.5
), and postulate linear approximations for the nuisance components
\xi
and
b
. Specifically, we assume that

\displaystyle\xi(X_{i},T_{i})=\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{i},T_{i})^{\top}\gamma_{\xi}+e_{1i},\qquad\mathbb{E}[\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{i},T_{i})e_{1i}]=0,

\displaystyle b(X_{i},T_{i})=\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{i},T_{i})^{\top}\gamma_{b}+e_{2i},\qquad\mathbb{E}[\bar{\phi}_{k}(X_{i},T_{i})e_{2i}]=0,

where
e_{1i}
and
e_{2i}
are the best linear approximation error, and
(\gamma_{\xi},\gamma_{b})
are the population least-squares coefficients, i.e.,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{k}^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}[\xi(X,T)\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(X)]=\Sigma_{k}^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(X)]

\displaystyle\gamma_{b}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{k}^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}[b(X,T)\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(X)]=\Sigma_{k}^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}[TY\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(X)].

Let
\widetilde{\xi}(X,T)=\bar{\phi}_{k}(X,T)^{\top}\gamma_{\xi}
and
\widetilde{b}(X,T)=\bar{\phi}_{k}(X,T)^{\top}\gamma_{b}
. Plugging
(\widetilde{\xi},\widetilde{b})
into the moment equation (
S5.5
) yields the following population quantity

\displaystyle\widetilde{\beta}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\xi}(X,T)\{Y-\widetilde{b}(X,T=1)\}+\widetilde{b}(X,T=1)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[T\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(X)Y\right]^{\top}\gamma_{\xi}-\gamma_{\xi}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[T\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(X)\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(X)^{\top}\right]\gamma_{b}+{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(X)]^{\top}\gamma_{b}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[T\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(X)Y\right]^{\top}\Sigma_{k}^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(X)]-\gamma_{\xi}^{\top}\Sigma_{k}\gamma_{b}+\gamma_{\xi}^{\top}\Sigma_{k}\gamma_{b}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[T\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(X)Y\right]^{\top}\Sigma_{k}^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{{\mathsf{z}}}_{k}(X)].

Hence, under this linear-approximation regime, the target parameter
\beta^{\ast}
actually reduces to the above
bilinear functional

\widetilde{\beta}
, which has exactly the same structure as
\mathrm{B}_{\psi,k}
(see e.g. (
10
)), which will also be reintroduced later specifically for ATE
\beta^{\ast}
.

In contrast, our higher-order construction instead targets the approximation bias of first-order estimating equation
